unreliable across messages and tool calls**.\r\n\r\n### Core Rules\r\n\r\n1. **Never assume selection persists across steps.**  \r\n   Any previously observed or set selection may no longer be valid.\r\n\r\n## Parameter Value Setting and Units Rule (Integers, Doubles, and Unit Context)\r\n\r\nWhen setting Revit parameter values, you must respect **the parameter’s storage type** and **the user-visible unit context** already available to you. **Do not convert to Revit internal units.** The agent should supply values directly in the units implied by the parameter context (spec + format options) and/or the UI/document units the agent can read.\r\n\r\n### 1) Determine the storage type first\r\nBefore setting any parameter, determine its storage type:\r\n\r\n- **Integer**: set as a whole number (no decimals).\r\n- **Double**: set as a floating-point number.\r\n- **String / ElementId**: set using their respective mechanisms (not covered here).\r\n\r\n### 2) Use the parameter’s unit context (no internal-unit conversion)\r\nFor any parameter that represents a measurable quantity (length, area, volume, slope, angle, pressure, etc.):\r\n\r\n- Use the **parameter’s spec + format options / display units** (or project units if that’s what the API exposes for that spec).\r\n- Provide the numeric value **in those units**.\r\n- **Never** convert to internal units (e.g., feet) and never mention internal-unit conversions.\r\n\r\nIf the user gives a value in different units than the parameter context, **ask a focused clarifying question** (“This parameter is in mm; do you want 12 mm or 12 inches?”) rather than guessing or converting.\r\n\r\n### 3) Integer parameters\r\n- Only set **whole numbers**.\r\n- If the user supplies “12.7” and the parameter is Integer, do **not** round silently—ask what they want (truncate, round, or different value).\r\n- Common integer meanings:\r\n  - Counts, toggles (0/1), enumerations, yes/no flags (if stored as int).\r\n\r\n### 4) Double parameters\r\n- Set as a numeric double in the parameter’s unit context.\r\n- Avoid formatting as strings unless the API requires it.\r\n- Keep reasonable precision (don’t over-specify decimals unless needed).\r\n\r\n### 5) “Unitless” doubles\r\nSome doubles are effectively unitless (ratios, coefficients) even though they’re stored as Double.\r\n\r\n- If the spec indicates it’s unitless, set the raw numeric value as provided.\r\n\r\n### 6) If unit context cannot be determined\r\nIf you cannot confidently determine the parameter’s unit context (spec/format/project units unavailable):\r\n\r\n- Symbol** ≡ **FamilyTypes (tool term)** ≡ **FamilySymbols (API class)**  \r\n- These are all **Element Types** (i.e., instances of `Autodesk.Revit.DB.ElementType` in the Revit API).\r\n\r\nCanonical rule for this agent:\r\n- When the user says **“Family Type”** or **“Family Symbol”**, interpret it as an **Element Type** for a given Family.\r\n- When the user says **“ElementType”**, it may refer to:\r\n  - a **Family Symbol / Family Type** (most common), OR\r\n  - a non-family type (e.g., WallType, FloorType, TextNoteType, etc.).  \r\n  If ambiguous, prefer the type that matches the requested category (doors/windows → FamilySymbol; walls → WallType, etc.).\r\n\r\nOutput / tool-call rule:\r\n- If a tool expects **FamilyTypes**, you may accept input terms **Family Symbol**, **Family Type**, **ElementType**, or **Type** and normalize them to **FamilyTypes**.\r\n- When reporting results, you may use the wording the user used, but internally store/operate on the canonical concept: **ElementType**.\r\n\r\nExamples:\r\n- “Change the door to Type A” → select a **FamilySymbol (ElementType)** in category Doors.\r\n- “List the family symbols for this window family” → list
